[2310.14772] Predictor-Rejector Multi-Class Abstention:Theoretical Analysis and Algorithms \altauthor Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences, New York and Google Research and Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences, New York and Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences, New York Predictor-Rejector Multi-Class Abstention: Theoretical Analysis and Algorithms Anqi Mao Email: aqmao@cims.nyu.edu Affiliation: Mehryar Mohri Email: mohri@google.com Affiliation: Yutao Zhong Email: yutao@cims.nyu.edu Affiliation: Abstract We study the key framework of learning with abstention in the multi-class classification setting. In this setting, the learner can choose to abstain from making a prediction with some pre-defined cost. We present a series of new theoretical and algorithmic results for this learning problem in the predictor-rejector framework. We introduce several new families of surrogate losses for which we prove strong non-asymptotic and hypothesis set-specific consistency guarantees, thereby resolving positively two existing open questions. These guarantees provide upper bounds on the estimation error of the abstention loss function in terms of that of the surrogate loss. We analyze both a single-stage setting where the predictor and rejector are learned simultaneously and a two-stage setting crucial in applications, where the predictor is learned in a first stage using a standard surrogate loss such as cross-entropy. These guarantees suggest new multi-class abstention algorithms based on minimizing these surrogate losses. We also report the results of extensive experiments comparing these algorithms to the current state-of-the-art algorithms on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100 and SVHN datasets. Our results demonstrate empirically the benefit of our new surrogate losses and show the remarkable performance of our broadly applicable two-stage abstention algorithm. keywords abstention, learning to abstain, consistency, learning theory 1 Introduction The problem of learning with abstention has become increasingly crucial in various applications. In natural language generation or question-answering, it is not always possible to provide accurate or factual responses. Therefore, it is of utmost importance to develop the ability to abstain from generating a response in such cases to prevent the occurrence of misleading or incorrect information, often referred to as hallucinations ( Wei et al. 2022 ; Filippova 2020 ; Maynez et al. 2020 ) . Abstention plays a critical role in several other application areas. For instance, in the field of autonomous vehicle control, an incorrect prediction can pose a significant threat to human lives. Similarly, in decision-making systems, incorrect choices can have severe ethical implications. These scenarios highlight the necessity of incorporating abstention mechanisms to ensure the safety, reliability, and ethical soundness of the learning process. We can distinguish several broad methods for learning with abstention in the literature: confidence-based methods , which consist of abstaining when the score returned by a pre-trained model falls below some threshold ( Chow 1957 ; Chow 1970 ; Bartlett and Wegkamp 2008 ; Yuan and Wegkamp 2010 ; Yuan and Wegkamp 2011 ; Ramaswamy et al. 2018 ; Ni et al. 2019 ) ; selective classification , which analyzes a set-up with a predictor and a selector and defines a selection risk or loss normalized by the expected selection or coverage ( El-Yaniv et al. 2010 ; Wiener and El-Yaniv 2011 ; El-Yaniv and Wiener 2012 ; Wiener and El-Yaniv 2015 ; Geifman and El-Yaniv 2017 ; Geifman and El-Yaniv 2019 ) ; a predictor-rejector formulation , which is based on learning both a predictor and a rejector , each from a different family of functions, and that takes into account explicitly the abstention cost c c ( Cortes et al. 2016a ; Cortes et al. 2016b ; Cortes et al. 2023 ; Cheng et al. 2023 ; Mohri et al. 2024 ; Li et al. 2024 ) ; and a more recent score-based formulation that consists of augmenting the multi-class categories with a rejection label and of abstaining when the score assigned to the rejection label is the highest ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ; Cao et al. 2022 ; Mao et al. 2024b ) . Another problem closely related to abstention is that of deferring to an alternative model, or even to a human in some instances. This can also be considered as a special case of the general abstention scenario and tackled in a similar way ( Madras et al. 2018 ; Raghu et al. 2019a ; Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ; Okati et al. 2021 ; Wilder et al. 2021 ; Verma and Nalisnick 2022 ; Narasimhan et al. 2022 ; Verma et al. 2023 ; Mao et al. 2023a ; Cao et al. 2023 ; Mao et al. 2024a ; Chen et al. 2024 ; Mao et al. 2024d ) . We will be particularly interested in the predictor-rejector formulation, which explicitly models the cost of abstention. The selective classification of El-Yaniv et al. 2010 is also interesting, but it does not explicitly factor in the cost c c and is based on a distinct objective. Confidence-based methods are also very natural and straightforward, but they may fail when the predictor is not calibrated, a property that often does not hold. Additionally, they have been shown to be suboptimal when the predictor differs from the Bayes classifier ( Cortes et al. 2016a ) . The score-based formulation ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ) admits very common properties and also explicitly takes into account the rejection cost c c . We will compare the predictor-rejector formulation with the score-based one. We will show via an example that the predictor-rejector is more natural in some instances and will also compare the two formulations in our experiments. We further elaborate on the difference between the two formulations in Appendix D . How should the problem of multi-class classification with abstention be formulated and when is it appropriate to abstain? The extension of the results of Cortes et al. 2016a to multi-class classification was found to be very challenging by Ni et al. 2019 . In fact, these authors left the following as an open question: can we define Bayes-consistent surrogate losses for the predictor-rejector abstention formulation in the multi-class setting? This paper deals precisely with this topic: we present a series of new theoretical, algorithmic, and empirical results for multi-class learning with abstention in predictor-rejector formulation and, in particular, resolve this open question in a strongly positive way. For the score-based formulation, a surrogate loss function based on cross-entropy was introduced by Mozannar and Sontag 2020 , which was proven to be Bayes-consistent. Building upon this work, Cao et al. 2022 presented a more comprehensive collection of Bayes-consistent surrogate losses for the score-based formulation. These surrogate losses can be constructed using any consistent loss function for the standard multi-class classification problem. More recently, Mao et al. 2024b gave an extensive analysis of surrogate losses for the score-based formulation supported by ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency bounds . In a recent study, Mozannar et al. 2023 demonstrated that existing score-based surrogate losses for abstention are not realizable consistent with respect to the abstention loss, as defined by Long and Servedio 2013 . Instead, they proposed a novel surrogate loss that achieves realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency, provided that the sets of predictors ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and rejectors ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are closed under scaling . However, the authors expressed uncertainty regarding the Bayes-consistency of their proposed surrogate losses and left open the question of identifying abstention surrogate losses that are both consistent and realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} satisfy the scaling closure property . We address this open question by demonstrating that our newly proposed surrogate losses benefit from both Bayes-consistency and realizable consistency. We give a more comprehensive discussion of related work in Appendix A . We show in Section 3 that in some instances the optimal solution cannot be derived in the score-based formulation, unless we resort to more complex scoring functions. In contrast, the solution can be straightforwardly derived in the predictor-rejector formulation. In Section 4 , we present and analyze a new family of surrogate loss functions for multi-class abstention in the predictor-rejector formulation, first in the single-stage setting , where the predictor h h and the rejector r r are selected simultaneously, next in a two-stage setting , where first the predictor h h is chosen and fixed and subsequently the rejector r r is determined. The two-stage setting is crucial in many applications since the predictor h h is often already learned after a costly training of several hours or days. Re-training to ensure a simultaneous learning of h h and r r is then inconceivable due to its prohibitive cost. In the single-stage setting (Section 4.1 ), we first give a negative result, ruling out abstention surrogate losses that do not verify a technical condition. Next, we present several positive results for abstention surrogate losses verifying that condition, for which we prove non-asymptotic ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds ( Awasthi et al. 2022a ; Awasthi et al. 2022b ) that are stronger than Bayes-consistency. Next, in Section 4.2 , we also prove ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for the two-stage setting . Minimizing these new surrogate losses directly result in new algorithms for multi-class abstention. In Section 4.3 , we prove realizable consistency guarantees for both single-stage and two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses. In Section 5 , we empirically show that our two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss consistently outperforms state-of-the-art scored-based surrogate losses, while our single-stage one achieves comparable results. Our main contributions are summarized below: • Counterexample for score-based abstention formulation. • Negative results for single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses. • New families of single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses for which we prove strong non-asymptotic and hypothesis set-specific consistency guarantees, thereby resolving positively an open question mentioned by Ni et al. 2019 . • Two-stage predictor-rejector formulations and their ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency bounds guarantees. • Realizable consistency guarantees for both single-stage and two-stage surrogate losses, which resolve positively the recent open question posed by Mozannar et al. 2023 . • Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100 and SVHN datasets empirically demonstrating the usefulness of our proposed surrogate losses. 2 Preliminaries We first introduce some preliminary concepts and definitions, including the description of the predictor-rejector formulation and background on ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency bounds. We examine the standard multi-class classification scenario with an input space 𝒳 {\mathscr{X}} and a set of n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 classes or labels 𝒴 = { 1 , … , n } {\mathscr{Y}}=\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\} . We will denote by 𝒟 {\mathscr{D}} a distribution over 𝒳 × 𝒴 {\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} and by p ⁡ ( x , y ) = 𝒟 ⁡ ( Y = y ∣ X = x ) p(x,y)={\mathscr{D}}(Y=y\mid X=x) the conditional probability of Y = y Y=y given X = x X=x . We will also adopt the shorthand p ⁡ ( x ) = ( p ⁡ ( x , 1 ) , … , p ⁡ ( x , n ) ) p(x)=\left(p(x,1),\ldots,p(x,n)\right) to denote the vector of conditional probabilities, given x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} . We study more specifically the learning scenario of multi-class classification with abstention, within the predictor-rejector formulation introduced by Cortes et al. 2016b . Predictor-rejector formulation. In this formulation of the abstention problem, we have a hypothesis set ℋ {\mathscr{H}} of prediction functions mapping from 𝒳 × 𝒴 {\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} to ℝ \mathbb{R} , along with a family ℛ {\mathscr{R}} of abstention functions, or rejectors , which map from 𝒳 {\mathscr{X}} to ℝ \mathbb{R} . For an input x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} and a hypothesis h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} , the predicted label 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) {\sf h}(x) is defined as the one with the highest score, 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) = argmax y ∈ 𝒴 h ​ ( x , y ) {\sf h}(x)=\argmax_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}h(x,y) , using an arbitrary yet fixed deterministic strategy to break ties. A rejector r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} is used to abstain from predicting on input x x if r ⁡ ( x ) r(x) is non-positive, r ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 r(x)\leq 0 , at a cost c ⁡ ( x ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] c(x)\in[0,1] . The predictor-rejector abstention loss 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} for this formulation is thus defined for any ( h , r ) ∈ ℋ × ℛ (h,r)\in{\mathscr{H}}\times{\mathscr{R}} and ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒳 × 𝒴 (x,y)\in{\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} as 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r , x , y ) = 𝟙 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) ≠ y ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) &gt; 0 + c ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 . {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(h,r,x,y)=\mathds{1}_{{\sf h}(x)\neq y}\mathds{1}_{r(x)&gt;0}+c(x)\mathds{1}_{r(x)\leq 0}. (1) When the learner does not abstain ( r ⁡ ( x ) &gt; 0 r(x)&gt;0 ), it incurs the familiar zero-one classification loss. Otherwise, it abstains ( r ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 r(x)\leq 0 ) at the expense of a cost c ⁡ ( x ) c(x) . The learning problem consists of using a finite sample of size m m drawn i.i.d. from 𝒟 {\mathscr{D}} to select a predictor h h and rejector r r with small expected 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} loss, 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝒟 [ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r , x , y ) ] \E_{(x,y)\sim{\mathscr{D}}}[{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(h,r,x,y)] . For simplification, in the following, we assume a constant cost function c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) c\in(0,1) . This assumption is not necessary however, and all ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds results in Sections 4.1 and 4.2 extend straightforwardly to general cost functions. Optimizing the predictor-rejector abstention loss is intractable for most hypothesis sets. Thus, learning algorithms in this context must rely on a surrogate loss 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} for 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} . In the subsequent sections, we study the consistency properties of several surrogate losses. Given a surrogate loss function 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} in the predictor-rejector framework, we denote by ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) = 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝒟 [ 𝖫 ⁡ ( h , r , x , y ) ] {\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)=\E_{(x,y)\sim{\mathscr{D}}}\left[{\mathsf{L}}(h,r,x,y)\right] the 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} -expected loss of a pair ( h , r ) ∈ ℋ × ℛ (h,r)\in{\mathscr{H}}\times{\mathscr{R}} and by ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) = inf h ∈ ℋ , r ∈ ℛ ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) {\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})=\inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}},r\in{\mathscr{R}}}{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r) its infimum over ℋ × ℛ {\mathscr{H}}\times{\mathscr{R}} . ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds. We will prove ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for several surrogate losses in the predictor-rejector framework, which extend to the predictor-rejector framework the ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency bounds of Awasthi et al. 2021a ; Awasthi et al. 2021c ; Awasthi et al. 2022a ; Awasthi et al. 2022b ; Awasthi et al. 2023 ; Awasthi et al. 2024 ; Mao et al. 2023c ; Mao et al. 2023d ; Mao et al. 2023e ; Zheng et al. 2023 ; Mao et al. 2023b ; Mao et al. 2023f ; Mao et al. 2024e ; Mao et al. 2024c . These are inequalities upper-bounding the predictor-rejector abstention estimation loss 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} of a hypothesis h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} and rejector r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} with respect to their surrogate estimation loss. They admit the following form: ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ≤ f ⁡ ( ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ) , {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\leq f({\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})), where f f is a non-decreasing function. Thus, the estimation error ( ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ) ({\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})) is then bounded by f ⁡ ( ϵ ) f(\epsilon) if the surrogate estimation loss ( ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ) ({\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})) is reduced to ϵ \epsilon . An important term that appears in these bounds is the minimizability gap , which is defined by ℳ 𝖫 ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) = ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) − 𝔼 x [ inf h ∈ ℋ , r ∈ ℛ 𝔼 y [ 𝖫 ⁡ ( h , r , X , y ) ∣ X = x ] ] {\mathscr{M}}_{\mathsf{L}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})={\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})-\E_{x}\left[\inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}},r\in{\mathscr{R}}}\E_{y}[{\mathsf{L}}(h,r,X,y)\mid X=x]\right] . When the loss function 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} depends solely on h ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) h(x,\cdot) and r ⁡ ( x ) r(x) , which holds for most loss functions used in applications, and when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} include all measurable functions, the minimizability gap is null ( Steinwart 2007 , lemma 2.5) . However, it is generally non-zero for restricted hypothesis sets ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} . The minimizability gap can be upper-bounded by the approximation error 𝒜 𝖫 ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) = ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) − 𝔼 x [ inf h , r 𝔼 y [ 𝖫 ⁡ ( h , r , X , y ) ∣ X = x ] ] {\mathscr{A}}_{\mathsf{L}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})={\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})-\E_{x}\big[\inf_{h,r}\E_{y}[{\mathsf{L}}(h,r,X,y)\mid X=x]\big] , where the infimum is taken over all measurable functions. But, the minimizability gap is a finer quantity and leads to more favorable guarantees. 3 Counterexample for score-based abstention losses We first discuss a natural example here that can be tackled straightforwardly in the predictor-rejector formulation but for which the same solution cannot be derived in the score-based formulation setting, unless we resort to more complex functions. This natural example motivates our study of surrogate losses for the predictor-rejector formulation in Section 4 . We begin by discussing the score-based formulation and subsequently highlight its relationship with the predictor-rejector formulation. Score-based abstention formulation. In this version of the abstention problem, the label set 𝒴 {\mathscr{Y}} is expanded by adding an extra category ( n + 1 ) (n+1) , which represents abstention. We indicate the augmented set as 𝒴 ~ = { 1 , … , n , n + 1 } \widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}=\left\{1,\ldots,n,n+1\right\} and consider a hypothesis set ℋ ~ \widetilde{\mathscr{H}} comprising functions that map from 𝒳 × 𝒴 ~ {\mathscr{X}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}} to ℝ \mathbb{R} . The label assigned to an input x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} by h ~ ∈ ℋ ~ \widetilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}} is denoted as 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) \widetilde{\sf h}(x) . It is defined as 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) = n + 1 \widetilde{\sf h}(x)=n+1 if h ~ ​ ( x , n + 1 ) ≥ max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ h ~ ​ ( x , y ) \widetilde{h}(x,n+1)\geq\max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}\widetilde{h}(x,y) ; otherwise, 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) \widetilde{\sf h}(x) is determined as an element in 𝒴 {\mathscr{Y}} with the highest score, 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) = argmax y ∈ 𝒴 h ~ ​ ( x , y ) \widetilde{\sf h}(x)=\argmax_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}\widetilde{h}(x,y) , using an arbitrary yet fixed deterministic strategy to break ties. When 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) = n + 1 \widetilde{\sf h}(x)=n+1 , the learner chooses to abstain from predicting for x x and incurs a cost c c . In contrast, it predicts the label y = 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) y=\widetilde{\sf h}(x) if otherwise. The score-based abstention loss 𝖫 ~ abs {\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} for this formulation is defined for any h ~ ∈ ℋ ~ \widetilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}} and ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒳 × 𝒴 (x,y)\in{\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} as follows: 𝖫 ~ abs ​ ( h ~ , x , y ) = 𝟙 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) ≠ y ​ 𝟙 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) ≠ n + 1 + c ​ 1 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) = n + 1 . {\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(\widetilde{h},x,y)=\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\sf h}(x)\neq y}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\sf h}(x)\neq n+1}+c\,\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\sf h}(x)=n+1}. (2) Thus, as in the predictor-rejector context, when the learner does not abstain ( 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) ≠ n + 1 \widetilde{\sf h}(x)\neq n+1 ), it reduces to the familiar zero-one classification loss. Conversely, when it abstains ( 𝗁 ~ ​ ( x ) = n + 1 \widetilde{\sf h}(x)=n+1 ), it incurs the cost c c . With a finite sample drawn i.i.d. from 𝒟 {\mathscr{D}} , the learning problem involves choosing a hypothesis h ~ \widetilde{h} within ℋ ~ \widetilde{\mathscr{H}} that yields a minimal expected score-based abstention loss, 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝒟 [ 𝖫 ~ abs ​ ( h ~ , x , y ) ] \E_{(x,y)\sim{\mathscr{D}}}[{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(\widetilde{h},x,y)] . Relationship between the two formulations. In the score-based formulation, rejection is defined by the condition h ~ ​ ( x , n + 1 ) − max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ h ~ ​ ( x , y ) ≥ 0 \widetilde{h}(x,n+1)-\max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}\widetilde{h}(x,y)\geq 0 . Thus, a predictor-rejector formulation with ( h , r ) ∈ ℋ × ℛ (h,r)\in{\mathscr{H}}\times{\mathscr{R}} can be equivalently formulated as a score-based problem with h ~ \widetilde{h} defined by h ~ ​ ( x , y ) = h ​ ( x , y ) \widetilde{h}(x,y)=h(x,y) for y ∈ 𝒴 y\in{\mathscr{Y}} and h ~ ​ ( x , n + 1 ) = max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ h ⁡ ( x , y ) − r ⁡ ( x ) \widetilde{h}(x,n+1)=\max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}h(x,y)-r(x) : 𝖫 ~ abs ​ ( h ~ , x , y ) = 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r , x , y ) {\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(\widetilde{h},x,y)={{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(h,r,x,y) for all ( x , y ) × 𝒳 × 𝒴 (x,y)\times{\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} . Note, however, that function h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , n + 1 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,n+1) is in general more complex. As an example, while h h and r r may both be in a family of linear functions, in general, h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , n + 1 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,n+1) defined in this way is no more linear. Thus, a score-based formulation might require working with more complex functions. We further elaborate on the difference between the two formulations in Appendix D . Figure 1 : Counterexample for score-based abstention losses. Counterexample setting. Let 𝒴 = { 1 , 2 } {\mathscr{Y}}=\left\{1,2\right\} and let x x follow the uniform distribution on the unit ball 𝖡 2 ​ ( 1 ) = { x : ‖ x ‖ 2 ≤ 1 } {\mathsf{B}}_{2}(1)=\left\{x\colon\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq 1\right\} . We will consider the linear models f ∈ ℱ lin = { x → 𝐰 ⋅ x + 𝐛 ∣ ‖ 𝐰 ‖ 2 = 1 } f\in{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{lin}}=\left\{x\rightarrow{\mathbf{w}}\cdot x+{\mathbf{b}}\mid\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2}=1\right\} . We set the label of a point x x as follows: fix two linear functions f abs ​ ( x ) = 𝐰 abs ⋅ x + 𝐛 abs f_{\rm{abs}}(x)={\mathbf{w}}_{\rm{abs}}\cdot x+{\mathbf{b}}_{\rm{abs}} and f pred ​ ( x ) = 𝐰 pred ⋅ x + 𝐛 pred f_{\rm{pred}}(x)={\mathbf{w}}_{\rm{pred}}\cdot x+{\mathbf{b}}_{\rm{pred}} in ℱ lin {\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{lin}} , if f abs ​ ( x ) ≤ 0 f_{\rm{abs}}(x)\leq 0 , then set y = 1 y=1 with probability 1 2 \frac{1}{2} and y = 2 y=2 with probability 1 2 \frac{1}{2} ; if f abs ( x ) &gt; 0 and f pred ( x ) &gt; 0 f_{\rm{abs}}(x)&gt;0\text{ and }f_{\rm{pred}}(x)&gt;0 , then set y = 1 y=1 ; if f abs ( x ) &gt; 0 and f pred ( x ) ≤ 0 f_{\rm{abs}}(x)&gt;0\text{ and }f_{\rm{pred}}(x)\leq 0 , then set y = 2 y=2 ; see Figure 1 . We denote by ℋ lin {\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{lin}} the hypothesis set of linear scoring functions h h with two labels: h ⁡ ( ⋅ , 1 ) h(\cdot,1) and h ⁡ ( ⋅ , 2 ) h(\cdot,2) are in ℱ lin {\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}} with the natural constraint h ⁡ ( ⋅ , 1 ) + h ⁡ ( ⋅ , 2 ) = 0 h(\cdot,1)+h(\cdot,2)=0 as in Lee et al. 2004 . We also denote by ℋ ~ lin \widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{lin}} the linear hypothesis set of functions h ~ \widetilde{h} with three scores h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 1 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,1) , h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 2 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,2) , h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 3 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,3) in ℱ lin {\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}} with the same constraint h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 1 ) + h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 2 ) = 0 \widetilde{h}(\cdot,1)+\widetilde{h}(\cdot,2)=0 while h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 3 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,3) is independent of h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 1 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,1) and h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 2 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,2) . Note that here the constraint is imposed only to simplify the analysis and is not necessary for the counter-example to hold. Thus, for any cost c ∈ [ 0 , 1 2 ) c\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right) , the Bayes solution in this setting consists of abstaining on { x ∈ 𝖡 2 ​ ( 1 ) : f abs ​ ( x ) ≤ 0 } \left\{x\in{\mathsf{B}}_{2}(1):f_{\rm{abs}}(x)\leq 0\right\} and otherwise making a prediction according to the decision surface f pred ​ ( x ) = 0 f_{\rm{pred}}(x)=0 . In the predictor-rejector formulation, the learner seeks to select a hypothesis h h in ℋ lin {\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}} and a rejector r r in ℱ lin {\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}} with small expected predictor-rejector loss, 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝒟 [ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r , x , y ) ] \E_{(x,y)\sim{\mathscr{D}}}[{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(h,r,x,y)] . In the score-based abstention formulation, the learner seeks to select a hypothesis h ~ \widetilde{h} in ℋ ~ lin \widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}} with small expected score-based abstention loss, 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝒟 [ 𝖫 ~ abs ​ ( h ~ , x , y ) ] \E_{(x,y)\sim{\mathscr{D}}}[{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(\widetilde{h},x,y)] . We will show that, in the predictor-rejector formulation, it is straightforward to find the Bayes solution but, in the score-based formulation, the same solution cannot be achieved, unless a more complex family of functions is adopted for h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 3 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,3) . Predictor-rejector formulation succeeds. For the predictor-rejector abstention loss 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} , it is straightforward to see that for any cost c ∈ [ 0 , 1 2 ) c\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right) , the best-in-class predictor and rejector h ℋ lin ∗ h^{*}_{{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}} and r ℱ lin ∗ r^{*}_{{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}}} can be expressed as follows: h ℋ lin ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , 1 ) = f pred ​ ( ⋅ ) , h ℋ lin ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , 2 ) = − f pred ​ ( ⋅ ) , r ℱ lin ∗ = f abs h^{*}_{{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}(\cdot,1)=f_{\rm{pred}}(\cdot),\,h^{*}_{{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}(\cdot,2)=-f_{\rm{pred}}(\cdot),\,r^{*}_{{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}}}=f_{\rm{abs}} . Moreover, it is clear that h ℋ lin ∗ h^{*}_{{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}} and r ℱ lin ∗ r^{*}_{{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}}} match the Bayes solution. Score-based abstention formulation fails. For the score-based abstention loss 𝖫 ~ abs {\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} , for any cost c ∈ [ 0 , 1 2 ) c\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right) , the best-in-class classifier h ~ ℋ ~ lin ∗ \widetilde{h}^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}\!\! has the following form: h ~ ℋ ~ lin ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , 1 ) = f 1 ​ ( ⋅ ) , h ~ ℋ ~ lin ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , 2 ) = − f 1 ​ ( ⋅ ) , h ~ ℋ ~ lin ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , 3 ) = f 2 ​ ( ⋅ ) , \widetilde{h}^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}(\cdot,1)=f_{1}(\cdot),\,\widetilde{h}^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}(\cdot,2)=-f_{1}(\cdot),\,\widetilde{h}^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}(\cdot,3)=f_{2}(\cdot), for some f 1 , f 2 ∈ ℱ lin f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm{lin}} . Thus, h ~ ℋ ~ lin ∗ \widetilde{h}^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{lin}}}\!\! abstains from making a prediction on { x ∈ 𝖡 2 ​ ( 1 ) : f 2 ​ ( x ) ≤ | f 1 ​ ( x ) | } \left\{x\in{\mathsf{B}}_{2}(1):f_{2}(x)\leq\left\lvert f_{1}(x)\right\rvert\right\} and otherwise predicts according to the decision surface f 1 ​ ( x ) = 0 f_{1}(x)=0 . To match the Bayes solution, f 1 f_{1} must equal f pred f_{\rm{pred}} and f 2 f_{2} must satisfy the following condition: f 2 ​ ( x ) ≥ | f pred ​ ( x ) | ⇔ f abs ​ ( x ) ≤ 0 , f_{2}(x)\geq\left\lvert f_{\rm{pred}}(x)\right\rvert\Leftrightarrow f_{\rm{abs}}(x)\leq 0, which does not hold. Therefore, unless we resort to more complex functions for h ~ ​ ( ⋅ , 3 ) \widetilde{h}(\cdot,3) , the score-based formulation cannot result in the Bayes solution. 4 Predictor-rejector surrogate losses In this section, we present and analyze a new family of surrogate loss functions 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} for 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} , first in the single-stage setting , where the predictor h h and the rejector r r are selected simultaneously, next in a two-stage setting , where first the predictor h h is chosen and fixed and subsequently the rejector r r is determined. We give ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds and guarantees for both settings. 4.1 Single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses In view of the expression of the predictor-rejector abstention loss 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r , x , y ) = 𝟙 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) ≠ y ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) &gt; 0 + c ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}(h,r,x,y)=\mathds{1}_{{\sf h}(x)\neq y}\mathds{1}_{r(x)&gt;0}+c\mathds{1}_{r(x)\leq 0} , if ℓ \ell is a surrogate loss for the zero-one multi-class classification loss over the set of labels 𝒴 {\mathscr{Y}} , then, 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} defined as follows is a natural surrogate loss for 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} : for all ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒳 × 𝒴 (x,y)\in{\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} , 𝖫 ⁡ ( h , r , x , y ) = ℓ ⁡ ( h , x , y ) ​ Φ ​ ( − α ​ r ​ ( x ) ) + Ψ ⁡ ( c ) ​ Φ ​ ( β ​ r ​ ( x ) ) , \mspace{-0mu}{\mathsf{L}}(h,r,x,y)=\ell(h,x,y)\Phi\left(-\alpha r(x)\right)+\Psi(c)\Phi\left(\beta r(x)\right),\mspace{-5mu} (3) where Ψ \Psi is a non-decreasing function, Φ \Phi is a non-increasing auxiliary function upper bounding t ↦ 𝟙 t ≤ 0 t\mapsto\mathds{1}_{t\leq 0} and α \alpha , β \beta are positive constants. The formulation ( 3 ) of 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} is a multi-class generalization of the binary abstention surrogate loss proposed in ( Cortes et al. 2016b ; Cortes et al. 2016a ) , where the binary margin-based loss Φ ~ ​ ( y ​ h ​ ( x ) ) \widetilde{\Phi}(yh(x)) and Ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t \Psi(t)=t are used instead: 𝖫 bin ​ ( h , r , x , y ) = Φ ~ ​ ( y ​ h ​ ( x ) ) ​ Φ ​ ( − α ​ r ​ ( x ) ) + c ​ Φ ​ ( β ​ r ​ ( x ) ) . {\mathsf{L}}_{\mathrm{bin}}(h,r,x,y)=\widetilde{\Phi}(yh(x))\Phi\left(-\alpha r(x)\right)+c\Phi\left(\beta r(x)\right).\mspace{-1mu} (4) Minimizing 𝖫 bin {\mathsf{L}}_{\mathrm{bin}} with a regularization term was shown to achieve state-of-the-art results in the binary case with margin-based losses Φ ~ \widetilde{\Phi} such as the exponential loss Φ ~ exp ​ ( t ) = exp ⁡ ( − t ) \widetilde{\Phi}_{\rm{exp}}(t)=\exp(-t) and the hinge loss Φ ~ hinge ​ ( t ) = max ⁡ { 1 − t , 0 } \widetilde{\Phi}_{\rm{hinge}}(t)=\max\left\{1-t,0\right\} ( Cortes et al. 2016b ; Cortes et al. 2016a ) . However, we will show below that its multi-class generalization 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} imposes a more stringent condition on the choice of the surrogate loss ℓ \ell , which rules out for example the multi-class exponential loss. We will show, however, that several other loss functions do satisfy that condition, for example the multi-class hinge loss. In the following, for simplicity, we consider Φ ⁡ ( t ) = exp ⁡ ( − t ) \Phi(t)=\exp(-t) as in ( Cortes et al. 2016b ) for the main analysis, though a similar analysis can be given for other functions Φ \Phi . We first present a negative result, ruling out surrogate losses 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} that are based on a loss ℓ \ell that does not verify a certain condition. As with ( Awasthi et al. 2022b ) , we assume that the hypothesis sets are symmetric and complete . We say that a hypothesis set 𝒢 {\mathscr{G}} is symmetric if there exists a family ℱ {\mathscr{F}} of functions f f mapping from 𝒳 {\mathscr{X}} to ℝ \mathbb{R} such that { [ g ( x , 1 ) , … , g ( x , n ) ] : g ∈ 𝒢 } = { [ f 1 ( x ) , … , f n ( x ) ] : f 1 , … , f n ∈ ℱ } \left\{\left[g(x,1),\ldots,g(x,n)\right]\colon g\in{\mathscr{G}}\right\}=\left\{\left[f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{n}(x)\right]\colon f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in{\mathscr{F}}\right\} , for any x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} . We say that a hypothesis set ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is complete if the set of scores it generates spans ℝ \mathbb{R} , that is, { g ⁡ ( x , y ) : g ∈ 𝒢 } = ℝ \left\{g(x,y)\colon g\in{\mathscr{G}}\right\}=\mathbb{R} , for any ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒳 × 𝒴 (x,y)\in{\mathscr{X}}\times{\mathscr{Y}} . The hypothesis sets widely used in practice including linear models and multilayer feedforward neural networks are all symmetric and complete. Theorem 1 ( Negative result for single-stage surrogates ) . Assume that ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is symmetric and complete, and that ℛ {\mathscr{R}} is complete. If there exists x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} such that inf h ∈ ℋ 𝔼 y [ ℓ ⁡ ( h , X , y ) ∣ X = x ] ≠ β ​ Ψ ​ ( 1 − max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ p ⁡ ( x , y ) ) α \inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}}}\E_{y}\left[\ell(h,X,y)\mid X=x\right]\neq\frac{\beta\Psi\left(1-\max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}p(x,y)\right)}{\alpha} , then, there does not exist a non-decreasing function Γ : ℝ + → ℝ + \Gamma\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+} with the property lim t → 0 + Γ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 \lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\Gamma(t)=0 such that the following ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound holds: for all h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} , r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} , and any distribution, ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) + ℳ 𝖫 abs ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) + ℳ 𝖫 ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ) . {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\leq\Gamma\left({\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\right). The proof (Appendix G.1 ) proceeds by contradiction. Assuming that the ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound is valid would entail that the pointwise best-in-class predictor and best-in-class rejector for the single-stage surrogate loss align with those of the abstention loss, which can be characterized by Lemma 20 in Appendix F . Incorporating those explicit forms into the analysis of the conditional risk of the surrogate loss leads to a contradiction upon examination of its derivatives. In view of Theorem 1 , to find a surrogate loss 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} that admits a meaningful ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound, we need to consider multi-class surrogate losses ℓ \ell for which the following condition holds for any x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} , for some Ψ \Psi and pair ( α , β ) ∈ ℝ \plus 2 (\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{\plus}^{2} : inf h ∈ ℋ 𝔼 y [ ℓ ⁡ ( h , X , y ) ∣ X = x ] = β ​ Ψ ​ ( 1 − max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ p ⁡ ( x , y ) ) α . \displaystyle\inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}}}\E_{y}\left[\ell(h,X,y)\mid X=x\right]=\frac{\beta\Psi\left(1-\max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}p(x,y)\right)}{\alpha}. In the binary case, this condition is easily verifiable, as max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ p ⁡ ( x , y ) \max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}p(x,y) uniquely determines the other probabilities. However, in the multi-class scenario, a fixed max y ∈ 𝒴 ⁡ p ⁡ ( x , y ) \max_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}p(x,y) still allows for variation in the other probabilities within inf h ∈ ℋ 𝔼 y [ ℓ ⁡ ( h , X , y ) ∣ X = x ] \inf_{h\in{\mathscr{H}}}\E_{y}\left[\ell(h,X,y)\mid X=x\right] . This leads to the difficulties in extending the binary framework to the multi-class classification. Nevertheless, we will show that this necessary condition is satisfied by three common multi-class surrogate losses ℓ \ell . Furthermore, we will prove ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for the predictor-rejector surrogate losses 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} based on any of these three choices of ℓ \ell defined for all h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} and ( x , y ) (x,y) as follows: (i) The mean absolute error loss ( Ghosh et al. 2017 ) : ℓ mae ​ ( h , x , y ) = 1 − e h ⁡ ( x , y ) ∑ y ′ ∈ 𝒴 e h ⁡ ( x , y ′ ) \ell_{\rm{mae}}(h,x,y)=1-\frac{e^{h(x,y)}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{Y}}}e^{h(x,y^{\prime})}} ; (ii) The constrained ρ \rho -hinge loss : ℓ ρ − hinge ​ ( h , x , y ) = ∑ y ′ ≠ y Φ ρ − hinge ​ ( − h ⁡ ( x , y ′ ) ) \ell_{\rho-\mathrm{hinge}}(h,x,y)=\sum_{y^{\prime}\neq y}\Phi_{\rho-\rm{hinge}}\left(-h(x,y^{\prime})\right) , ρ &gt; 0 \rho&gt;0 , with Φ ρ − hinge ​ ( t ) = max ⁡ { 0 , 1 − t ρ } \Phi_{\rho-\rm{hinge}}(t)=\max\big\{0,1-\frac{t}{\rho}\big\} the ρ \rho -hinge loss, and the constraint ∑ y ∈ 𝒴 h ⁡ ( x , y ) = 0 \sum_{y\in{\mathscr{Y}}}h(x,y)=0 . (iii) The ρ \rho -Margin loss : ℓ ρ ​ ( h , x , y ) = Φ ρ ​ ( ρ h ​ ( x , y ) ) \ell_{\rho}(h,x,y)=\Phi_{\rho}\left(\rho_{h}(x,y)\right) , with ρ h ​ ( x , y ) = h ⁡ ( x , y ) − max y ′ ≠ y ⁡ h ⁡ ( x , y ′ ) \rho_{h}(x,y)=h(x,y)-\max_{y^{\prime}\neq y}h(x,y^{\prime}) the confidence margin and Φ ρ ​ ( t ) = min ⁡ { max ⁡ { 0 , 1 − t ρ } , 1 } , ρ &gt; 0 \Phi_{\rho}(t)=\min\big\{\max\big\{0,1-\frac{t}{\rho}\big\},1\big\},\rho&gt;0 the ρ \rho -margin loss. Theorem 2 ( ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for single-stage surrogates ) . Assume that ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is symmetric and complete and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} is complete. Then, for α = β \alpha=\beta , and ℓ = ℓ mae \ell=\ell_{\rm{mae}} , or ℓ = ℓ ρ \ell=\ell_{\rho} with Ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t \Psi(t)=t , or ℓ = ℓ ρ − hinge \ell=\ell_{\rho-\mathrm{hinge}} with Ψ ⁡ ( t ) = n ​ t \Psi(t)=nt , the following ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound holds for all h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} , r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} and any distribution: ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) + ℳ 𝖫 abs ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) + ℳ 𝖫 ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ) , {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\leq\Gamma\left({\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\right), where Γ ⁡ ( t ) = max ⁡ { 2 ​ n ​ t , n ​ t } \Gamma(t)=\max\left\{2n\sqrt{t},n\,t\right\} for ℓ = ℓ mae \ell=\ell_{\rm{mae}} ; Γ ⁡ ( t ) = max ⁡ { 2 ​ t , t } \Gamma(t)=\max\left\{2\sqrt{t},t\right\} for ℓ = ℓ ρ \ell=\ell_{\rho} ; and Γ ⁡ ( t ) = max ⁡ { 2 ​ n ​ t , t } \Gamma(t)=\max\left\{2\sqrt{nt},t\right\} for ℓ = ℓ ρ − hinge \ell=\ell_{\rho-\rm{hinge}} . The theorem provides strong guarantees for the predictor-rejector surrogate losses we described in the single-stage setting. The technique used in the proof (Appendix G.2 ) is novel and requires careful analysis of various cases involving the pointwise best-in-class predictor and rejector. This analysis is challenging and needs to take into account the conditional risk and calibration gap (see Appendix F ) of specific loss functions. The approach is substantially different from the standard scenarios examined in ( Awasthi et al. 2022b ) , due to the simultaneous minimization of both the predictor and rejector in the abstention setting. Discussions on Theorem 2 are given in Remark 14 in Appendix B . The following is a direct consequence of Theorem 2 when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} include all measurable functions, since the minimizability gaps ℳ 𝖫 abs {\mathscr{M}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}} and ℳ 𝖫 {\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathsf{L}}} are then zero. Corollary 3 ( Excess error bounds for single-stage surrogates ) . For α = β \alpha=\beta , and ℓ = ℓ mae \ell=\ell_{\rm{mae}} , or ℓ = ℓ ρ \ell=\ell_{\rho} with Ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t \Psi(t)=t , or ℓ = ℓ ρ − hinge \ell=\ell_{\rho-\mathrm{hinge}} with Ψ ⁡ ( t ) = n ​ t \Psi(t)=nt , the following excess error bound holds for all h ∈ ℋ all h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}} , r ∈ ℛ all r\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}} and any distribution: ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ all , ℛ all ) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ all , ℛ all ) ) , {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}},{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\leq\Gamma\left({\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}},{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\right), where Γ \Gamma has the same form as in Theorem 2 . The corollary resolves in a positive way the open question mentioned by Ni et al. 2019 . In fact it provides a stronger result since it gives non-asymptotic excess error bounds for the three abstention surrogate losses previously described. These are stronger guarantees than Bayes-consistency of these loss functions, which follow immediately by taking the limit. It should be noted that our novel single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses might present some challenges for optimization. This is due to several factors: the difficulty of optimizing the mean absolute error loss ( Zhang and Sabuncu 2018 ) (also see Section 5 ), the restriction imposed by the constrained hinge loss, which is incompatible with the standard use of the softmax function in neural network hypotheses, and the non-convexity of the ρ \rho -margin loss. However, our primary objective has been a theoretical analysis and the significance of these surrogate losses lies in their novelty and strong guarantees. As shown in Corollary 3 , they are the first Bayes-consistent surrogate losses within the predictor-rejector formulation for multi-class abstention, addressing an open question in the literature ( Ni et al. 2019 ) . 4.2 Two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses Here, we explore a two-stage algorithmic approach, for which we introduce surrogate losses with more flexible choices of ℓ \ell that admit better optimization properties, and establish ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for them. This is a key scenario since, in practice, often a large pre-trained prediction model is already available (first stage), and retraining it would be prohibitively expensive. The problem then consists of leaving the first stage prediction model unchanged and of subsequently learning a useful rejection model (second stage). Let ℓ \ell be a surrogate loss for standard multi-class classification and Φ \Phi a function like the exponential function that determines a margin-based loss Φ ⁡ ( y ​ r ​ ( x ) ) \Phi(yr(x)) in binary classification, with y ∈ { − 1 , + 1 } y\in\left\{-1,+1\right\} for a function r r . We propose a two-stage algorithmic approach and a surrogate loss to minimize in the second stage: first, find a predictor h h by minimizing ℓ \ell ; second, with h h fixed, find r r by minimizing ℓ Φ , h \ell_{\Phi,h} , a surrogate loss function of r r for all ( x , y ) (x,y) , defined by ℓ Φ , h ​ ( r , x , y ) = 𝟙 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) ≠ y ​ Φ ​ ( − r ⁡ ( x ) ) + c ​ Φ ​ ( r ⁡ ( x ) ) , \displaystyle\ell_{\Phi,h}\left(r,x,y\right)=\mathds{1}_{{\sf h}(x)\neq y}\Phi\left(-r(x)\right)+c\Phi\left(r(x)\right), (5) where t ↦ Φ ⁡ ( t ) t\mapsto\Phi(t) is a non-increasing auxiliary function upper bounding 𝟙 t ≤ 0 \mathds{1}_{t\leq 0} . This algorithmic approach is straightforward since the first stage involves the familiar task of finding a predictor using a standard surrogate loss, such as logistic loss (or cross-entropy with softmax). The second stage is also relatively simple as h h remains fixed and the form of ℓ Φ , h \ell_{\Phi,h} is uncomplicated, with Φ \Phi possibly being the logistic or exponential loss. It is important to underscore that the judicious selection of the indicator function in the initial term of ( 5 ) plays a crucial role in guaranteeing that the two-stage surrogate loss benefits from ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds. If a surrogate loss is used in the first stage, this may not necessarily hold. Note that in ( 5 ), h h is fixed and only r r is learned by minimizing the surrogate loss corresponding to that h h , while in contrast both h h and r r are jointly learned in the abstention loss ( 1 ). We denote by ℓ abs , h \ell_{\mathrm{abs},h} the two-stage version of the abstention loss ( 1 ) with a fixed predictor h h , defined as: for any r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} , x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} and y ∈ 𝒴 y\in{\mathscr{Y}} : ℓ abs , h ​ ( r , x , y ) = 𝟙 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) ≠ y ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) &gt; 0 + c ​ 𝟙 r ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 . \ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}(r,x,y)=\mathds{1}_{{\sf h}(x)\neq y}\mathds{1}_{r(x)&gt;0}+c\mathds{1}_{r(x)\leq 0}. (6) In other words, both ℓ Φ , h \ell_{\Phi,h} and ℓ abs , h \ell_{\mathrm{abs},h} are loss functions of the abstention function r r , while 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} is a loss function of the pair ( h , r ) ∈ ( ℋ , ℛ ) (h,r)\in({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) . Define the binary zero-one classification loss as ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( r , x , y ) = 𝟙 y ≠ sign ⁡ ( r ⁡ ( x ) ) \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}(r,x,y)=\mathds{1}_{y\neq\sign(r(x))} , where sign ⁡ ( t ) = 𝟙 t &gt; 0 − 𝟙 t ≤ 0 \sign(t)=\mathds{1}_{t&gt;0}-\mathds{1}_{t\leq 0} . As with the single-stage surrogate losses, the two-stage surrogate losses benefit from strong consistency guarantees as well. We first show that in the second stage where a predictor h h is fixed, the surrogate loss function ℓ Φ , h \ell_{\Phi,h} benefits from ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bounds with respect to ℓ abs , h \ell_{\mathrm{abs},h} if, Φ \Phi admits an ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bound with respect to the binary zero-one loss ℓ 0 − 1 binary \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}} . Theorem 4 ( ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bounds for second-stage surrogates ) . Fix a predictor h h . Assume that Φ \Phi admits an ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 binary \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}} . Thus, there exists a non-decreasing concave function Γ \Gamma such that, for all r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} , ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( ℛ ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}({\mathscr{R}}) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ℰ Φ ​ ( r ) − ℰ Φ ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ Φ ​ ( ℛ ) ) . \displaystyle\leq\Gamma\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\Phi}({\mathscr{R}})\right). Then, the following ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bound holds for all r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} and any distribution: ℰ ℓ abs , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ abs , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ ℓ abs , h ​ ( ℛ ) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ( ℰ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ Φ , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( ℛ ) ) / c ) . {\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}}({\mathscr{R}})\leq\Gamma\left(\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}({\mathscr{R}})\right)/c\right). The proof (Appendix G.3 ) consists of analyzing the calibration gap of the abstention loss and second-stage surrogate loss, for a fixed predictor h h . The calibration gap here is more complex than that in the standard setting as it takes into account the conditional probability, the error of that fixed predictor and the cost, and thus requires a completely different analysis. To establish ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bounds, we need to upper bound the calibration gap of the abstention loss by that of the surrogate loss. However, directly working with them is rather difficult due to their complex forms. Instead, a key observation is that both forms share structural similarities with the calibration gaps in the standard classification. Motivated by the above observation, we construct an appropriate conditional distribution to transform the two calibration gaps into standard ones. Then, by applying Lemma 21 in Appendix F , we manage to leverage the ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bound of Φ \Phi with respect to the binary zero-one classification loss to upper bound the target calibration gap by that of the surrogate calibration gap. We further discuss Theorem 4 in Remark 15 (Appendix B ). In the special case where ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are the family of all measurable functions, all the minimizability gap terms in Theorem 4 vanish. Thus, we obtain the following corollary. Corollary 5 . Fix a predictor h h . Assume that Φ \Phi admits an excess error bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 binary \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}} . Thus, there exists a non-decreasing concave functions Γ \Gamma such that, for all r ∈ ℛ all r\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}} , ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}}) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ℰ Φ ​ ( r ) − ℰ Φ ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) ) . \displaystyle\leq\Gamma\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\right). Then, the following excess error bound holds for all r ∈ ℛ all r\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}} and any distribution: ℰ ℓ abs , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ abs , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) ≤ Γ ⁡ ( ( ℰ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ Φ , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) ) / c ) . {\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\mathrm{abs},h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\leq\Gamma\left(\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\right)/c\right). See Remark 16 (Appendix B ) for a brief discussion of Corollary 5 . We now present ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for the whole two-stage approach with respect to the abstention loss function 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} . Let ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} be the multi-class zero-one loss: ℓ 0 − 1 ​ ( h , x , y ) = 1 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ) ≠ y \ell_{0-1}(h,x,y)=1_{{\sf h}(x)\neq y} . We will consider hypothesis sets ℛ {\mathscr{R}} that are regular for abstention , that is such that for any x ∈ 𝒳 x\in{\mathscr{X}} , there exist f , g ∈ ℛ f,g\in{\mathscr{R}} with f ⁡ ( x ) &gt; 0 f(x)&gt;0 and g ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 g(x)\leq 0 . If ℛ {\mathscr{R}} is regular for abstention, then, for any x x , there is an option to accept and an option to reject. Theorem 6 ( ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds for two-stage approach ) . Suppose that ℛ {\mathscr{R}} is regular. Assume that ℓ \ell admits an ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} and that Φ \Phi admits an ℛ {\mathscr{R}} -consistency bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 binary \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}} . Thus, there are non-decreasing concave functions Γ 1 \Gamma_{1} and Γ 2 \Gamma_{2} such that, for all h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} and r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} , ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) + ℳ ℓ 0 − 1 ​ ( ℋ ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{0-1}}({\mathscr{H}}) ≤ Γ 1 ​ ( ℰ ℓ ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) + ℳ ℓ ​ ( ℋ ) ) \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{1}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}^{*}({\mathscr{H}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell}({\mathscr{H}})\right) ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( ℛ ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}({\mathscr{R}}) ≤ Γ 2 ​ ( ℰ Φ ​ ( r ) − ℰ Φ ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ Φ ​ ( ℛ ) ) . \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\Phi}({\mathscr{R}})\right). Then, the following ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound holds for all h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} , r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} and any distribution: ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) + ℳ 𝖫 abs ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) ≤ Γ 1 ​ ( ℰ ℓ ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) + ℳ ℓ ​ ( ℋ ) ) \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{1}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}^{*}({\mathscr{H}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell}({\mathscr{H}})\right) + ( 1 + c ) ​ Γ 2 ​ ( ( ℰ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ Φ , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ ) + ℳ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( ℛ ) ) / c ) , \displaystyle\quad+(1+c)\Gamma_{2}\left(\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}})+{\mathscr{M}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}({\mathscr{R}})\right)/c\right), where the constant factors ( 1 + c ) (1+c) and 1 c \frac{1}{c} can be removed when Γ 2 \Gamma_{2} is linear. In the proof (Appendix G.4 ), we express the pointwise estimation error term for the target abstention loss as the sum of two terms. The first term represents the pointwise estimation error of the abstention loss with a fixed h h , while the second term denotes that with a fixed r ∗ r^{*} . This proof is entirely novel and distinct from the approach used for a standard loss without abstention in ( Awasthi et al. 2022b ) . Discussions on Theorem 6 are given in Remark 17 in Appendix B . As before, when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are the family of all measurable functions, the following result on excess error bounds holds. Corollary 7 . Assume that ℓ \ell admits an excess error bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} and that Φ \Phi admits an excess error bound with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 binary \ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}} . Thus, there are non-decreasing concave functions Γ 1 \Gamma_{1} and Γ 2 \Gamma_{2} such that, for all h ∈ ℋ all h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}} and r ∈ ℛ all r\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}} , ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ∗ ​ ( ℋ all ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}}) ≤ Γ 1 ​ ( ℰ ℓ ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ ∗ ​ ( ℋ all ) ) \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{1}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}})\right) ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 binary ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}^{\rm{binary}}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}}) ≤ Γ 2 ​ ( ℰ Φ ​ ( r ) − ℰ Φ ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) ) . \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\Phi}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\right). Then, the following excess error bound holds for all h ∈ ℋ all h\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}} and r ∈ ℛ all r\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}} and any distribution: ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ all , ℛ all ) \displaystyle{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}},{\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}}) ≤ Γ 1 ​ ( ℰ ℓ ​ ( h ) − ℰ ℓ ∗ ​ ( ℋ all ) ) + ( 1 + c ) ​ Γ 2 ​ ( ( ℰ ℓ Φ , h ​ ( r ) − ℰ ℓ Φ , h ∗ ​ ( ℛ all ) ) / c ) , \displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{1}\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}(h)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}^{*}({\mathscr{H}}_{\rm{all}})\right)+(1+c)\Gamma_{2}\left(\left({\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}(r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,h}}^{*}({\mathscr{R}}_{\rm{all}})\right)/c\right), where the constant factors ( 1 + c ) (1+c) and 1 c \frac{1}{c} can be removed when Γ 2 \Gamma_{2} is linear. See Remark 18 (Appendix B ) for a brief discussion of Corollary 7 . These results provide a strong guarantee for surrogate losses in the two-stage setting. Additionally, while the choice of ℓ \ell in the single-stage setting was subject to certain conditions, here, the multi-class surrogate loss ℓ \ell can be chosen more flexibly. Specifically, it can be selected as the logistic loss (or cross-entropy with softmax), which is not only easier to optimize but is also better tailored for complex neural networks. In the second stage, the formulation is straightforward, and the choice of function Φ \Phi is flexible, resulting in a simple smooth convex optimization problem with respect to the rejector function r r . Moreover, the second stage simplifies the process as h h remains constant and only the rejector is optimized. This approach can enhance optimization efficiency. In Appendix C , we further highlight the significance of our findings regarding the two-stage formulation in comparison with single-stage surrogate losses. 4.3 Other advantages of the predictor-rejector formulation In this section, we prove another advantage of our predictor-rejector surrogate losses, that is realizable consistency ( Long and Servedio 2013 ; Zhang and Agarwal 2020 ) . The property involves ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -realizable distributions, under which there exist h ∗ ∈ ℋ h^{*}\in{\mathscr{H}} and r ∗ ∈ ℛ r^{*}\in{\mathscr{R}} such that ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h ∗ , r ∗ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h^{*},r^{*})=0 . The realizable distribution allows the optimal solution to abstain on points where the cost c ⁡ ( x ) c(x) is zero. Definition 8 . 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} is realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent with respect to 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} if, for any ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -realizable distribution, lim n → + ∞ ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h n , r n ) = ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ⟹ lim n → + ∞ ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h n , r n ) = ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) \lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h_{n},r_{n})={\mathscr{E}}^{*}_{{\mathsf{L}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\implies\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h_{n},r_{n})={\mathscr{E}}^{*}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) . In the following, we will establish that our predictor-rejector surrogate losses, both the single-stage and two-stage variants, are realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are closed under scaling . A hypothesis set 𝒢 {\mathscr{G}} is closed under scaling if, g ∈ 𝒢 g\in{\mathscr{G}} implies that ν ​ g \nu g is also in 𝒢 {\mathscr{G}} for any ν ∈ ℝ \nu\in\mathbb{R} . We will adopt the following mild assumption for the auxiliary function Φ \Phi in the predictor-rejector surrogate losses. Assumption 1 . For any t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} , Φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 𝟙 t ≤ 0 \Phi(t)\geq\mathds{1}_{t\leq 0} and lim t → \plus ​ ∞ Φ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 \lim_{t\to\plus\infty}\Phi(t)=0 . In other words, Φ \Phi upper bounds the indicator function and approaches zero as t t goes to infinity. We first prove a general result showing that single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses are realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent with respect to 𝖫 abs {\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}} if the adopted ℓ \ell satisfies Assumption 2 . Assumption 2 . When h ⁡ ( x , y ) − max y ′ ≠ y ⁡ h ⁡ ( x , y ′ ) &gt; 0 h(x,y)-\max_{y^{\prime}\neq y}h(x,y^{\prime})&gt;0 , lim ν → \plus ​ ∞ ℓ ⁡ ( ν ​ h , x , y ) = 0 \lim_{\nu\to\plus\infty}\ell(\nu h,x,y)=0 and ℓ ≥ ℓ 0 − 1 \ell\geq\ell_{0-1} . The assumption implies that for a sample ( x , y ) (x,y) for which a predictor h h achieves zero error, the infimum value of the loss function ℓ \ell is zero for any hypothesis set ℋ {\mathscr{H}} that includes the predictor h h , provided that ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is closed under scaling. Theorem 9 . Assume that ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are closed under scaling. Let Ψ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 \Psi(0)=0 and Φ \Phi satisfy Assumption 1 . Then, for any ℓ \ell that satisfies Assumption 2 , the following ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bound holds for any ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -realizable distribution, h ∈ ℋ h\in{\mathscr{H}} and r ∈ ℛ r\in{\mathscr{R}} : ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) ≤ ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) . {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}). The proof is included in Appendix G.5 . We first establish upper bounds for ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) {\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) using the optimal predictor and rejector for the abstention loss, subsequently expressing the upper bound as the sum of two terms. By applying the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we show that both terms vanish, and thus ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}})=0 . It is worth noting that ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds in Theorem 2 imply the realizable-consistency for considered loss functions. This is because under the realizable assumption, the minimizability gaps vanish for these loss functions. Nevertheless, Theorem 9 proves that a more general family of loss functions can actually achieve realizable consistency. According to Theorem 9 , for any distribution that is ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -realizable, minimizing the single-stage surrogate estimation loss ℰ 𝖫 ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) {\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{\mathsf{L}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) results in the minimization of the abstention estimation loss ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h , r ) − ℰ 𝖫 abs ∗ ​ ( ℋ , ℛ ) {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(h,r)-{\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}^{*}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) . This suggests that the single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss functions are realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent. In particular, when ℓ \ell is chosen as 2 ​ ℓ mae 2\ell_{\rm{mae}} , ℓ = ℓ ρ \ell=\ell_{\rho} and ℓ = ℓ ρ − hinge \ell=\ell_{\rho-\mathrm{hinge}} as suggested in Section 4.1 , Assumption 1 is satisfied. Thus, we obtain the following corollary. Corollary 10 . Under the same assumption as in Theorem 9 , for ℓ = 2 ​ ℓ mae \ell=2\ell_{\rm{mae}} , ℓ = ℓ ρ \ell=\ell_{\rho} and ℓ = ℓ ρ − hinge \ell=\ell_{\rho-\mathrm{hinge}} , the single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate 𝖫 {\mathsf{L}} is realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent with respect to 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} . Next, we prove a similar result showing that the two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses are realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent with respect to 𝖫 abs {\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}} if the multi-class surrogate loss ℓ \ell is realizable ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistent with respect to the multi-class zero-one loss ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is closed under scaling. Definition 11 . We say that ℓ \ell is realizable ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistent with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} if, for any distribution such that ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}^{*}_{\ell_{0-1}}({\mathscr{H}})=0 , lim n → + ∞ ℰ ℓ ​ ( h n ) = ℰ ℓ ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) ⟹ lim n → + ∞ ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ​ ( h n ) = ℰ ℓ 0 − 1 ∗ ​ ( ℋ ) = 0 \lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell}(h_{n})={\mathscr{E}}^{*}_{\ell}({\mathscr{H}})\implies\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{0-1}}(h_{n})={\mathscr{E}}^{*}_{\ell_{0-1}}({\mathscr{H}})=0 . Theorem 12 . Assume that ℋ {\mathscr{H}} and ℛ {\mathscr{R}} are closed under scaling. Let ℓ \ell be any multi-class surrogate loss that is realizable ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistent with respect to ℓ 0 − 1 \ell_{0-1} when ℋ {\mathscr{H}} is closed under scaling and Φ \Phi satisfies Assumption 1 . Let h ^ \hat{h} be the minimizer of ℰ ℓ {\mathscr{E}}_{\ell} and r ^ \hat{r} be the minimizer of ℰ ℓ Φ , h ^ {\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,\hat{h}}} . Then, for any ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -realizable distribution, ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h ^ , r ^ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(\hat{h},\hat{r})=0 . The proof is included in Appendix G.6 . We first establish the upper bound ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h ^ , r ^ ) ≤ ℰ ℓ Φ , h ^ ​ ( r ^ ) {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(\hat{h},\hat{r})\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,\hat{h}}}(\hat{r}) . Next, we analyze two cases: whether abstention occurs or not. By applying the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we show that ℰ ℓ Φ , h ^ ​ ( r ^ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}_{\ell_{\Phi,\hat{h}}}(\hat{r})=0 in both cases, consequently leading to ℰ 𝖫 abs ​ ( h ^ , r ^ ) = 0 {\mathscr{E}}_{{{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}}}(\hat{h},\hat{r})=0 . By Theorem 12 , under the realizability assumption, minimizing a two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss leads to zero abstention loss. This implies that the two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss functions are also realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent. Kuznetsov et al. 2014 prove the realizable ℋ {\mathscr{H}} -consistency of a broad family of multi-class surrogate losses including the logistic loss commonly used in practice. Thus, we obtain the following corollary. Corollary 13 . Under the same assumption as in Theorem 12 , for ℓ \ell being the logistic loss, the two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss is realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistent with respect to 𝖫 abs {{\mathsf{L}}_{\rm{abs}}} . Note that existing score-based abstention surrogate losses were shown to be not realizable consistent in ( Mozannar et al. 2023 ) . Recall that in Section 4.1 and Section 4.2 , the ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds guarantees (applicable to all distributions without any assumptions) indicate that both our single-stage and two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses are also Bayes-consistent, while it is unknown if the surrogate loss proposed by Mozannar et al. 2023 is. By combining the results from Section 4.1 , Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 , we demonstrate the advantages of the predictor-rejector formulation. As a by-product of our results, we address two open questions in the literature ( Ni et al. 2019 ) and ( Mozannar et al. 2023 ) indicated in Section 1 . 5 Experiments Table 1 : Abstention loss of our predictor-rejector surrogate losses against baselines: the state-of-the-art score-based abstention surrogate losses in ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ; Cao et al. 2022 ) . Dataset Method Abstention loss SVHN ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ) 1.61% ± \pm 0.06% ( Cao et al. 2022 ) 2.16% ± \pm 0.04% single-stage predictor-rejector ( ℓ mae \ell_{\rm{mae}} ) 2.22% ± \pm 0.01% two-stage predictor-rejector 0.94% ​ ± \pm 0.02% CIFAR-10 ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ) 4.48% ± \pm 0.10% ( Cao et al. 2022 ) 3.62% ± \pm 0.07% single-stage predictor-rejector ( ℓ mae \ell_{\rm{mae}} ) 3.64% ± \pm 0.05% two-stage predictor-rejector 3.31% ​ ± \pm 0.02% CIFAR-100 ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ) 10.40% ± \pm 0.10% ( Cao et al. 2022 ) 14.99% ± \pm 0.01% single-stage predictor-rejector ( ℓ mae \ell_{\rm{mae}} ) 14.99% ± \pm 0.01% two-stage predictor-rejector 9.23% ​ ± \pm 0.03% In this section, we present experimental results for the single-stage and two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses, as well as for the state-of-the-art score-based abstention surrogate losses ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ; Cao et al. 2022 ) on three popular datasets: SVHN ( Netzer et al. 2011 ) , CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 ( Krizhevsky 2009 ) . Note that the basic confidence-based approach has already been shown in ( Cao et al. 2022 ) to be empirically inferior to state-of-the-art score-based abstention surrogate losses. More details on the experiments are included in Appendix E . Results. In Table 1 , we report the mean and standard deviation of the abstention loss over three runs for our algorithms and the baselines. Table 1 shows that our two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art score-based abstention surrogate losses in ( Mozannar and Sontag 2020 ; Cao et al. 2022 ) across all cases. The single-stage predictor-rejector surrogate loss with ℓ \ell set as the mean absolute error loss achieves comparable results. Our predictor-rejector surrogate losses, both the single-stage and two-stage variants, benefit from ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds and realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency guarantees. While the optimization of mean absolute error loss is known to be challenging, as highlighted in the study by Zhang et al. (2018), our two-stage algorithm sidesteps this hurdle since it can use the more tractable logistic loss. 6 Conclusion We presented a series of theoretical, algorithmic, and empirical results for multi-class classification with abstention. Our theoretical analysis, including proofs of ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency bounds and realizable ( ℋ , ℛ ) ({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{R}}) -consistency, covers both single-stage and two-stage predictor-rejector surrogate losses. These results further provide valuable tools applicable to the analysis of other loss functions in learning with abstention. Our two-stage algorithmic approach provides practical and efficient solutions for multi-class abstention across various tasks. This approach proves particularly advantageous in scenarios where a large pre-trained prediction model is readily available, and the expense associated with retraining is prohibitive. Our empirical findings corroborate the efficacy of these algorithms, further reinforcing their practical usefulness. Additionally, our work reveals some limitations of the score-based abstention formulation, such as its inability to consistently yield optimal solutions in certain cases. In contrast, we present a collection of positive outcomes for various families of predictor-rejector surrogate loss functions. Importantly, our findings also provide resolutions to two open questions within the literature. We believe that our analysis and the novel loss functions we introduced can guide the design of algorithms across a broad spectrum of scenarios beyond classification with abstention.
